<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"`.</multi_agent_role>

A short definition alone is not a lesson. Avoid recursive definitions and tongue twisters.\\r\\n- Break explanations into readable sections with clear visual hierarchy, larger text, generous spacing, and short paragraphs. Inspect the actual rendered page, including the docked browser width and mobile, rather than treating JSX inspection as visual approval.\\r\\n- Bring meaningful interaction early, after enough teaching to attempt it. Feedback explains the reasoning and distinguishes given facts from conclusions of a rule. Do not merely cycle reveal buttons and call that practice.\\r\\n- Use color, emphasis, and motion to direct attention to the entire relevant idea. Preserve complete conditions, restrictions, timing, modality, and the difference between a rule and a fact.\\r\\n- Preserve existing teaching media while revising. Check that videos still exist and play in their intended lesson. New media must earn its place in the teaching flow.\\r\\n- Review content against the corpus, review the rendered UI, and use independent Claude Code review when requested. Record the actual model from the run, review artifact, findings, disposition, and limitations. Code/screenshot review is not live browser testing. Tests, local build, deployment, and signed-in acceptance are separate claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Necessary-condition lesson: explicitly approved wording\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua supplied and approved this copy on 2026-10-11:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **A necessary condition is a requirement. It has to be met, but meeting it might not be enough.**\\r\\n> For example, you have to be a student to be enrolled in law school. That makes being a student **necessary for law-school enrollment**.\\r\\n> But being a student doesn’t mean you’re enrolled in law school. You could be studying history instead.\\r\\n> **Being a student is required. It doesn’t establish enrollment on its own.**\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep “might not”: a condition may be both necessary and sufficient. Do not replace this with “something another condition can’t be true without.”\\r\\n\\r\\nExplicit page corrections:\\r\\n- Emphasize the complete consequent, “then you are a student,” and identify “you are a student” as the condition. Do not teach isolated “then” as a standalone necessary-condition indicator.\\r\\n- Law student / history student / not a student should be vertical case tabs. Keep keyboard navigation, visible selected state, and mobile stacking. This is a decision for this case comparison, not a mandate for every interaction.\\r\\n- Remove “Replay emphasis”; use a gentle continuous pulse for this page, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. This does not authorize constant pulsing on every page.\\r\\n- Make clear which facts are given and which follow. If a history case says not enrolled, make it explicitly given rather than inferring it from studying history.\\r\\n\\r\\nContent preservation checks for the conditional sequence:\\r\\n- Necessary examples retain only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must; explain their meaning in the complete sentence and preserve modality/scope. This is the existing lesson coverage, not a context-free replacement dictionary.\\r\\n- Sufficient Group 1 stays on-page: if; when/whenever; any/anytime; all; every/every time; in order to; people who; each.\\r\\n- Keep the reference-letter qualifiers, including current and accepted for review. A distractor should reveal a reasoning error, not reward guessing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Must arrow: correction and open work\\r\\n\\r\\n- Confirmed title: **Must arrow**, not “Read the must arrow.”\\r\\n- Teach the direction explicitly with a familiar relationship: in this convention sufficient on the left, necessary on the right, arrow pointing right. The left condition guarantees the right **if the left condition is true**. The arrow alone asserts neither condition as a fact, neither causation nor chronological order, and no reverse inference.\\r\\n- The lead “A must arrow shows what has to be true if another condition is true” was rejected as too abstract on 2026-10-11. Do not preserve it as approved wording. Joshua wants the direction shown and explained directly.\\r\\n- Current provisional implementation: “The must arrow points from left to right. If the left side is true, the right side must be true.” The new LSAT Lab clip (jeDnykOrswY) supports certainty at 0:31 and left requiring right at 8:19, per the source review in this session. This wording is not yet user-approved.\\r\\n- Source comparison completed; final wording remains provisional pending Joshua’s review. Earlier assistant summaries saying tests passed or an opening was updated are implementation reports, not user approval.\\r\\n- Source finding, not a user wording approval: original HTML `raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html`, lines 322-356, supports sufficient-to-necessary placement. The parent source comparison is recorded in `brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-11-must-arrow-review.md`.\\r\\n- Source pointers, relative to the vault resolved through its AGENTS.md: `wiki/sources/lsat-lab-conditional-logic-clip.md` and `corpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_reference_conditionals.md#the-must-arrow-shows-a-direction-of-guarantee`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Must-arrow visual readback correction, 2026-10-11\\r\\n\\r\\nStatus: explicit newest user direction, relayed in this chat; resulting markup is provisional until reviewed by Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Label the diagram\'s **Left side** and **Right side** clearly.\\r\\n- Teach how to follow/read this arrow from left to right, and explain **must** as what must be true if the left condition is true.\\r\\n- Simplify or remove the repeated closing student conclusion. Prefer one intuitive readback of the diagram over restating the same inference in several paragraphs.\\r\\n- Scope: the original sufficient-to-necessary arrow in this introduction. This does not ban a later contrapositive or other valid inference that has been separately taught.\\r\\n\\r\\nAcceptance: inspect the rendered diagram for visible side labels; read its explanation aloud in order to check that direction and certainty are clear without repeated student conclusions. Preserve the condition that the left side is true. Do not call new markup user-approved merely because it implements this request.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Must-arrow layout and practice corrections, 2026-10-11\\r\\n\\r\\nStatus: six explicit browser corrections relayed in this chat. Do not keep card-count machinery merely because it already exists.\\r\\n- Remove the confusing “Think before you turn” framing and the corner “Explain in own words” / “Turn explanation” controls from this practice presentation.\\r\\n- Resolve the ambiguous “Revisit card” action. Labels should make the next action and its learning purpose obvious.\\r\\n- Add a clear progress bar and a polished sequence through the practice. Kept on this shelf is the complete sufficient condition.\', \'Notebook alone would cover every notebook. Keep the restriction: kept on this shelf.\']}/>\\r\\n    <p>In the next drill, find the indicator, select the complete condition, place it beside the arrow and check that your diagram keeps the rule\'s meaning.</p>\\r\\n  </NoteSection>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction FourRules() {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section\\"><div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><PopQuizTitle/></div><RuleDrill/></section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction ContrapositiveNote() {\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"What a contrapositive is\\">\\r\\n      <p>If the sufficient condition happens, the necessary condition <strong>must</strong> happen. The left side of the arrow guarantees the right side.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The contrapositive is what you know when the right side of the arrow goes missing:</p>\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ct-definition\\"><strong>If the <span className=\\"ct-hl\\">necessary condition</span> is absent, the <span className=\\"ct-hl\\" data-tone=\\"amber\\">sufficient condition</span> is absent too.</strong></p>\\r\\n      <p>Take this rule: <strong>If the ceiling light is on, then the power must be on.</strong> With no power, the light can\'t be on. No power, no light. As a rule: <strong>If the power is off, then the ceiling light must be off.</strong></p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"You already knew this\\">\\r\\n      <p>The contrapositive was inside the original rule the whole time, waiting for you to spot it. That\'s why the two are <PulseWord>logically equivalent</PulseWord>: if one is true, the other is true, and you can use either. They\'re <PunchWord>interchangeable!</PunchWord></p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Watch the lesson\\">\\r\\n      <ContrapositiveLecture/>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"How to write it\\">\\r\\n      <p>Write the rule with an arrow: <strong>Light → Power</strong>. The sufficient condition goes on the left side of the arrow. The necessary condition goes on the right side of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The contrapositive is <strong><Neg>Power</Neg> → <Neg>Light</Neg></strong>. A line through an idea means “not.” You get there in two steps:</p>\\r\\n      <ol className=\\"ct-moves\\">\\r\\n        <li><strong>Step 1: Flip the sides.</strong> The right side goes on the left, and the left side goes on the right.</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Step 2: Negate both.</strong> Put “not” on each idea. If an idea already has a “not,” the two cancel: a double negative turns positive.</li>\\r\\n      </ol>\\r\\n      <FlipAndNegateDemo/>\\r\\n      <p>Same two steps when a side already has a “not.” Watch what happens to it:</p>\\r\\n      <DoubleNegativeDemo/>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"What it is not\\">\\r\\n      <p>Two look-alikes fail. Wrong answer choices use them on purpose.</p>\\r\\n      <ThreeMoves/>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Read it, don\'t always write it\\">\\r\\n      <p>You can read a rule backward without writing anything: go from right to left and put a “not” on everything. Write the contrapositive when it helps you see the rule or connect it to another rule.</p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction IndicatorNote({ group }: { group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\' }) {\\r\\n  const flashcards = useContext(CourseFlashcardUrl);\\r\\n  const content = groupSections(group);\\r\\n  const expressions = content.slice(2, 9);\\r\\n  return <><NoteSection title={group === \'group-1\' ? \'Which cases does the rule cover?\' : \'What does the rule require?\'}>\\r\\n    <p>{group === \'group-1\' ? \'You know what a sufficient condition does. Now find the complete condition in differently worded rules. Keep every restriction on who, what, where or when before you put that condition on the left.\' : \'You know what a necessary condition does. Now read the requirement in each sentence, even when it appears first. Keep its full meaning before you put it on the right.\'}</p>\\r\\n    <p>Read each example, make the decision, then use the feedback to check your reasoning.</p>\\r\\n    {flashcards && <Link className=\\"oc-action\\" to={flashcardUrl(flashcards, group)}>Go to flashcards</Link>}\\r\\n  </NoteSection>\\r\\n    {expressions}\\r\\n    {group === \'group-2\' && <><OnlyAndOnlyIf/><OnlyAfter/></>}\\r\\n    <FamilySupplement group={group}/>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// Teach placement and reading through the familiar relationship, then require a decision.\\r\\nfunction MustArrowNote() {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section cr-course-section ma-lesson\\" data-reference-source={8}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>What the arrow says</h2></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-step-body\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ma-lead\\">An arrow joins the two conditions in a rule. It says one thing: <strong>if the condition on the left is true, the condition on the right must be true.</strong></p>\\r\\n      <figure className=\\"ma-example\\" aria-label=\\"If you are enrolled in law school, you must be a student\\">\\r\\n        <figcaption className=\\"ma-read-instruction\\">Read it as a sentence: <strong>“If you are enrolled in law school, then you must be a student.”</strong></figcaption>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"ma-relationship\\">\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-condition\\"><span className=\\"ma-side\\"><small>Left side</small>Sufficient condition</span><strong>You are enrolled in law school</strong><span className=\\"ma-meaning\\">Enough to guarantee the right side</span></div>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><span>must</span><svg viewBox=\\"0 0 100 32\\" fill=\\"none\\"><path d=\\"M3 16H93M77 3L94 16L77 29\\" stroke=\\"currentColor\\" strokeWidth=\\"5\\" strokeLinecap=\\"round\\" strokeLinejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg></div>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-condition\\"><span className=\\"ma-side\\"><small>Right side</small>Necessary condition</span><strong>You are a student</strong><span className=\\"ma-meaning\\">Required when the left side is true</span></div>\\r\\n        </div>\\r\\n      </figure>\\r\\n      <p>Being enrolled in law school is <strong>enough</strong> to know you are a student. That is why enrollment goes on the <strong>left</strong>. Being a student is <strong>required</strong> for enrollment, so it goes on the <strong>right</strong>. The arrow points from what is enough to what is required.</p>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-where\\" aria-label=\\"Where to start reading\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Start at the left</h3>\\r\\n        <p>A rule begins where the arrow begins. Say “if” and read the left condition. Then say “then” and read the right one: <strong>If you are enrolled in law school, then you are a student.</strong></p>\\r\\n        <p>Reading from the right says something else. “You are a student, so you are enrolled” is not what this rule says. The arrow only runs one way, and you start at its tail.</p>\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-purpose\\" aria-label=\\"What the arrow is for\\">\\r\\n        <h3>What the arrow is for</h3>\\r\\n        <p>A rule can take a full sentence to say. The arrow shows it in one line, so you can test a fact against it without rereading the sentence.</p>\\r\\n        <ul>\\r\\n          <li>You learn the <strong>left</strong> side is true. The right side must be true too.</li>\\r\\n          <li>You learn the <strong>right</strong> side is true. The left side stays open.</li>\\r\\n        </ul>\\r\\n        <p>This matters on the LSAT because arguments turn on which way a rule runs. A conclusion that follows the arrow is supported. A conclusion that runs against it needs something the rule never gave you.</p>\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-order\\" aria-label=\\"Two ways to say the same thing\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Two ways to say the same thing</h3>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>If</strong> you are enrolled in law school, you are a student.</p><p>You are a student <strong>if</strong> you are enrolled in law school.</p></div>\\r\\n        <p>Both mean: <strong>You are enrolled in law school → You are a student.</strong> Enrollment is sufficient, so it goes left, even when it comes last in the sentence.</p>\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-placement\\" aria-label=\\"Place and read the conditions\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Place the conditions. Then read the arrow.</h3>\\r\\n        <p>Choose what belongs on each side, then use the same direction with a new rule.</p>\\r\\n        <p className=\\"ma-compact-note\\">Put the <strong>condition</strong> beside the arrow, not the indicator word. For example, write “you are enrolled in law school,” not “if.”</p>\\r\\n        <MustArrowPractice />\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ma-takeaway\\"><strong>The arrow guarantees the right side when the left side is true.</strong> Being a student alone does not prove that you are enrolled in law school.</p>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension ma-reverse\\"><summary>Why can\'t you read it from right to left?</summary><div className=\\"ma-reverse-body\\"><p>A person could be a history student who is not enrolled in law school. That possibility fits our rule.</p><p>The arrow states a rule. By itself, it does not tell us that either condition is true, or that one causes the other.</p></div></details>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// One translation process, checked four ways, instead of five restarted lessons.\\r\\nfunction WholeMeaningNote() {\\r\\n  const onlyComparison = groupSection(\'group-2\', \'Compare only with the only\');\\r\\n  const insideCheck = Children.toArray(reference(12).props.children);\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check a translation four ways\\">\\r\\n      <p>A diagram can point the right way and still say the wrong thing. Each check below catches a different slip in the same translation process.</p>\\r\\n      <ol>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Group:</strong> did you find the whole idea the indicator points to, wherever it sits?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Qualification:</strong> did your short label keep every restriction?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Direction:</strong> did a similar-looking indicator trick you into the wrong side?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Imagined case:</strong> does your arrow claim something the rule leaves open?</li>\\r\\n      </ol>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 1: find the whole group, wherever it sits\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator points to a complete group, not a single word. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all points to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The group often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these rules in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The group comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable group rule. Ask which complete idea the rule calls required, then give that idea its role.</p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 2: keep every qualification\\">\\r\\n      <p>Once you have the group, shorten it without losing what limits it. These two sentences test the same habit from opposite sides of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\">\\r\\n        <div><blockquote>Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.</blockquote><p>Applicant alone isn\'t the condition. The rule is about an applicant who submits late. Drop that restriction and your diagram makes a claim about on-time applicants too.</p></div>\\r\\n        <div><blockquote>Entry requires a current pass.</blockquote><p>A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. A shorter label should save writing, not change the requirement.</p></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. What goes left?\\" options={[\'Applicant\', \'Applicant submits late\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You dropped the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would cover every applicant, including someone who submits on time. The sufficient idea is applicant submits late.\', \'This keeps the complete sufficient idea. The rule covers applicants who submit late, so keep that restriction with the applicant.\']}/>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Entry requires a current pass. What goes right?\\" options={[\'Has current pass\', \'Has any pass\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.\', \'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the rule requires.\']}/>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 3: only and the only point to different groups\\">{onlyComparison.props.children}</NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Can, should and must keep their meaning\\">\\r\\n      <blockquote>If a visitor registers, the visitor may enter.</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>Registration guarantees <strong>permission to enter</strong>. It does not mean the visitor actually enters. Keep “may enter” together on the right.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>If a report contains an error, the editor should correct it.</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>The rule tells us what the editor <strong>should do</strong>. It does not prove that the correction happens. A diagram must keep the obligation instead of turning it into a fact.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Keep time limits too. <strong>“Before noon”</strong> is not the same as “at some point.” When two ideas form one condition, keep both: <strong>“signed and dated”</strong> is the complete requirement.</p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Short labels stand for complete conditions\\">\\r\\n      <p>You choose the short labels. Know what each one means so a short diagram keeps the whole rule.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>All registered guests receive a wristband.</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p><strong>Registered</strong> means the guest is registered. <strong>Wristband</strong> means the guest receives a wristband.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Registered\\" right=\\"Wristband\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Read it back: <strong>If a guest is registered, the guest receives a wristband.</strong> A label like “Guest” would drop the word that matters. “Registered” keeps it.</p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 4: try the rule from the inside\\">{insideCheck.slice(0, -1)}</NoteSection>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst freshCases = [\\r\\n  { rule: \'If it rains on Saturday, the market moves into the town hall.\', questions: [\\r\\n    { prompt: \\"It\'s Friday. You\'ve read this rule and nothing else. Where will the market be on Saturday?\\", options: [\'In the town hall.\', \'Outdoors, as usual.\', \\"The rule alone doesn\'t settle it.\\"], correct: 2, feedback: [\'You treated the rule as a weather report. It tells you what follows if it rains. Nobody has told you that it will rain.\', \\"You assumed it won\'t rain. The rule hasn\'t told you that either, so the market\'s location is still open.\\", \\"The rule tells you what follows if it rains. It doesn\'t report that it rained.\\"] },\\r\\n    { prompt: \\"Saturday arrives, and it\'s raining. Now where is the market?\\", options: [\'In the town hall.\', \'Still not settled.\'], correct: 0, feedback: [\'Now you have a fact, not just a rule. Rain on Saturday meets the condition, so the rule puts the market in the town hall.\', \\"The fact changes things. The rule covers a rainy Saturday, and that\'s what we have, so the market\'s location is settled.\\"] },\\r\\n  ] },\\r\\n  { rule: \'Every student going on the museum trip has a signed permission slip.\', questions: [\\r\\n    { prompt: \'Which description matches this rule?\', options: [\'Going on the trip is enough to know a student has a signed slip.\', \'Having a signed slip is enough to know a student is going on the trip.\', \'Each one is enough to know the other.\'], correct: 0, feedback: [\'Pick any student on the trip: the rule says they have a signed slip. Going is enough to prove the slip, and the slip is what every student on the trip is required to have.\', \\"You made the requirement enough. A student could have a signed slip and stay at school with a cold. That student doesn\'t break the rule.\\", \\"The rule gives only one direction. Going on the trip proves a signed slip. A student with a slip who stays behind fits the rule, so the slip doesn\'t prove going.\\"] },\\r\\n  ] },\\r\\n  { rule: \'If the café runs out of bread, it closes early.\', questions: [\\r\\n    { prompt: \'Today the café did not run out of bread. Did it close early?\', options: [\'No. It stayed open until the usual time.\', \'Yes. It closed early.\', \'Not settled. It could stay open or close early for another reason.\'], correct: 2, feedback: [\'You treated running out of bread as the only reason to close early. The rule gives one reason. A café that closed early because of a power cut would still fit it.\', \\"Nothing here proves an early closing. The rule tells us what happens when the bread runs out, and today it didn\'t.\\", \\"Given that fact, the rule doesn\'t settle whether the café closed early. It could have stayed open, or closed early for another reason. Both cases fit the rule.\\"] },\\r\\n  ] },\\r\\n];\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"49c63e","wall_time_seconds":0.3941672,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":88,"output":"805:        : \\"A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\\"}</p>\\n"}'}]

Path \'\', line 3681, position 1.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"baf4ab","wall_time_seconds":1.9305954,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2597,"output":"import { useContext, type ReactNode } from \'react\';\\r\\nimport { Link } from \'react-router-dom\';\\r\\nimport { useLessonInteraction } from \'../courseProgress\';\\r\\nimport { CourseFlashcardUrl, flashcardUrl } from \'./FlashcardTools\';\\r\\nimport { Check, RotateCcw, X } from \'lucide-react\';\\r\\nimport { Pill, PopQuizTitle, PulseWord } from \'./ContrapositiveVisuals\';\\r\\nimport { QuickQuiz, type QuizItem } from \'./SufficientQuiz\';\\r\\nimport { SufficientConditionFilm } from \'./SufficientConditionFilm\';\\r\\nimport { necessaryConditionFilms } from \'../content/necessaryConditionFilms\';\\r\\nimport \'./necessary-note.css\';\\r\\n\\r\\ntype Part = string | { text: string; role: \'sufficient\' | \'necessary\' };\\r\\ntype FindDemo = { label: string; parts: Part[]; ifIdea: string; thenIdea: string; result: string; captions: [string, string, string] };\\r\\n\\r\\nconst lawSchool: FindDemo = {\\r\\n  label: \'Find the requirement in the law-school rule\',\\r\\n  parts: [\'You have to be \', { text: \'a student\', role: \'necessary\' }, \' to be \', { text: \'enrolled in law school\', role: \'sufficient\' }, \'.\'],\\r\\n  ifIdea: \'Enrolled in law school\',\\r\\n  thenIdea: \'A student\',\\r\\n  result: \'If you’re enrolled in law school, then you’re a student.\',\\r\\n  captions: [\'This is the rule. Click Step 1.\', \'Being a student is what the rule requires. Now click Step 2.\', \'The requirement goes after “then.” What requires it goes after “if.”\'],\\r\\n};\\r\\n\\r\\nconst fuel: FindDemo = {\\r\\n  label: \'A rule that names the requirement first\',\\r\\n  parts: [{ text: \'Fuel\', role: \'necessary\' }, \' is necessary for \', { text: \'the car to start\', role: \'sufficient\' }, \'.\'],\\r\\n  ifIdea: \'The car starts\',\\r\\n  thenIdea: \'It has fuel\',\\r\\n  result: \'If the car starts, then it has fuel.\',\\r\\n  captions: [\'This rule names the requirement first. Click Step 1.\', \'Fuel is the requirement, even though it comes first. Now click Step 2.\', \'Fuel still goes after “then.” Its place in the sentence doesn’t decide its role.\'],\\r\\n};\\r\\n\\r\\n/** Step 1 sweeps a highlight across the requirement in the sentence. Step 2 drops both ideas into an if-then frame. */\\r\\nfunction FindTheRequirement({ label, parts, ifIdea, thenIdea, result, captions }: FindDemo) {\\r\\n  const [stage, setStage] = useLessonInteraction(`requirement:${label}`, 0);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"ns-find\\" data-stage={stage} role=\\"group\\" aria-label={label}>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"ns-find-rule\\"><span>The rule</span>{parts.map((part, index) => typeof part === \'string\' ? part : <mark key={index} className=\\"ns-mark\\" data-role={part.role} data-lit={part.role === \'necessary\' ? stage >= 1 : stage >= 2}>{part.text}</mark>)}</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"ns-frame\\" data-show={stage >= 2} aria-hidden={stage < 2}>\\r\\n      <span className=\\"ns-frame-word\\">If</span><span className=\\"ns-slot\\" data-role=\\"sufficient\\">{ifIdea}</span>\\r\\n      <span className=\\"ns-frame-word\\">then</span><span className=\\"ns-slot\\" data-role=\\"necessary\\">{thenIdea}</span>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    {stage >= 2 && <p className=\\"ns-find-result\\"><span>Read it as</span>{result}</p>}\\r\\n    <p className=\\"ns-find-caption\\" role=\\"status\\">{captions[stage]}</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"ct-flip-controls\\">\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"ct-step\\" data-ready={stage === 0} data-done={stage >= 1} disabled={stage !== 0} onClick={() => setStage(1)}><b aria-hidden=\\"true\\">{stage >= 1 ? <Check size={16} strokeWidth={3}/> : 1}</b>Step 1. Click here to find the requirement</button>\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"ct-step\\" data-ready={stage === 1} data-done={stage >= 2} disabled={stage !== 1} onClick={() => setStage(2)}><b aria-hidden=\\"true\\">{stage >= 2 ? <Check size={16} strokeWidth={3}/> : 2}</b>Step 2. Click here to put it after “then”</button>\\r\\n      {stage > 0 && <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"ct-flip-reset\\" onClick={() => setStage(0)}><RotateCcw size={15} aria-hidden=\\"true\\"/>Start over</button>}\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst groupTwoWords = [\'only\', \'only if\', \'only when\', \'only where\', \'always\', \'requires\', \'must\'];\\r\\n\\r\\n/** The one look-alike that fails beside the reading that holds. */\\r\\nfunction RequiredNotEnough() {\\r\\n  const cards: { verdict: \'wrong\' | \'right\'; how: string; left: string; leftTone: \'amber\' | \'teal\'; right: string; rightTone: \'amber\' | \'teal\' }[] = [\\r\\n    { verdict: \'wrong\', how: \'Treat the requirement as enough.\', left: \'Student\', leftTone: \'teal\', right: \'Enrolled\', rightTone: \'amber\' },\\r\\n    { verdict: \'right\', how: \'Enrollment guarantees the requirement.\', left: \'Enrolled\', leftTone: \'amber\', right: \'Student\', rightTone: \'teal\' },\\r\\n  ];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"ns-cards\\">{cards.map(card => <article key={card.verdict} className=\\"ct-move\\" data-verdict={card.verdict}>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"ct-move-badge\\">{card.verdict === \'right\' ? <Check size={16} strokeWidth={3} aria-hidden=\\"true\\"/> : <X size={16} strokeWidth={3} aria-hidden=\\"true\\"/>}{card.verdict === \'right\' ? \'Right\' : \'Wrong\'}</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"ct-move-how\\">{card.how}</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"ct-move-diagram\\" aria-label={`${card.left} → ${card.right}`}><Pill text={card.left} tone={card.leftTone} negated={false}/><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span><Pill text={card.right} tone={card.rightTone} negated={false}/></p>\\r\\n  </article>)}</div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst quiz: QuizItem[] = [\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'Every bike in the race has working brakes.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Which idea is required?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'In the race\', tone: \'amber\', reason: \'Being in the race guarantees the brakes. It isn’t the requirement.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Working brakes\', tone: \'teal\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Any bike\', tone: \'amber\', reason: \'The rule only covers bikes in the race.\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 1,\\r\\n    answer: \'If a bike is in the race, then it has working brakes.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'Every bike in the race has working brakes.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Kai’s bike has working brakes. Is it in the race?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'Yes, it’s in the race.\', reason: \'Brakes are required, not enough. Plenty of bikes with good brakes never race.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'No, it isn’t.\', reason: \'The rule doesn’t rule the race out either.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Nothing settles it. It may or may not be in the race.\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 2,\\r\\n    answer: \'If a bike has working brakes, the rule doesn’t say whether it’s in the race.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'Only travelers who have shown a passport board this flight.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Which idea is required?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'Show a passport\', tone: \'teal\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Board the flight\', tone: \'amber\', reason: \'Boarding is what needs the passport.\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 0,\\r\\n    answer: \'If you board the flight, then you showed a passport.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'Flour is necessary to bake this bread.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Which idea goes after “then”?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'Bake this bread\', tone: \'amber\', reason: \'Baking is what needs the flour, so it goes after “if.”\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Flour\', tone: \'teal\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 1,\\r\\n    answer: \'If you bake this bread, then you have flour.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n];\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction Section({ title, children }: { title: string; children: ReactNode }) {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section\\"><div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}</section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n/** The Necessary Condition page, built to the Contrapositive page\'s shape. */\\r\\nexport function NecessaryNote() {\\r\\n  const flashcards = useContext(CourseFlashcardUrl);\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"What a necessary condition is\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ct-definition\\"><strong>A necessary condition is a requirement. It has to be met, but meeting it might not be enough.</strong></p>\\r\\n      <p>You have to be a student to be enrolled in law school. Being a student is <strong>necessary</strong> for law-school enrollment. But being a student doesn’t prove you’re enrolled. You could be studying history.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Where the certainty is\\">\\r\\n      <p><strong>The necessary condition is what you can guarantee will occur if the sufficient condition happens.</strong></p>\\r\\n      <p>If someone is enrolled in law school, being a student has to be true. Enrollment is enough to prove that requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The sufficient condition opens the door. Once it\'s open, the necessary condition is guaranteed to be on the other side. That makes it <PulseWord>the place of certainty.</PulseWord> It\'s what you can prove.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Watch the lesson\\">\\r\\n      <SufficientConditionFilm assets={necessaryConditionFilms} title=\\"Why the requirement is not enough\\" ariaLabel=\\"Short explanation of necessity\\"/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"How to find it\\">\\r\\n      <ol className=\\"ct-moves\\">\\r\\n        <li><strong>Step 1: Find the requirement.</strong> Ask what has to be true if the other idea happens.</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Step 2: Put it after “then.”</strong> The idea that requires it goes after “if.”</li>\\r\\n      </ol>\\r\\n      <FindTheRequirement {...lawSchool}/>\\r\\n      <p>Same two steps when the rule names the requirement first:</p>\\r\\n      <FindTheRequirement {...fuel}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Words that mark the requirement\\">\\r\\n      <p>In the fuel rule, <strong>necessary</strong> marked the requirement. These Group 2 words and phrases mark it too:</p>\\r\\n      <ul className=\\"ns-words\\">{groupTwoWords.map(word => <li key={word}>{word}</li>)}</ul>\\r\\n      <p>The indicator is the word. The necessary condition is the whole idea it points to.</p>\\r\\n      {flashcards && <Link className=\\"oc-action\\" to={flashcardUrl(flashcards, \'group-2\', undefined, \'necessary\')}>Go to flashcards</Link>}\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"What it is not\\">\\r\\n      <p>Meeting a requirement might not be enough. Wrong answer choices count on you treating it as a guarantee.</p>\\r\\n      <RequiredNotEnough/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Picture it on its own\\">\\r\\n      <p>Once the rule identifies a requirement, picture that requirement met while the sufficient condition is false. A history student who is not enrolled in law school. A car with fuel and a dead battery. These cases fit their rules. They show why meeting those requirements does not prove enrollment or a running car.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <section className=\\"co-section\\"><div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><PopQuizTitle/></div><QuickQuiz items={quiz} idPrefix=\\"nq\\"/></section>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]

When that sufficient condition is absent, the other condition may still be true.</p>\\n756:        <p>We call getting a T-shirt the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. Here it is a required result of signing up, not a task you must complete before signing up.</p>\\n768:        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\n772:          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\\n785:        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If I get a T-shirt, must I have signed up for the fun run?\\">\\n793:        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We\'ll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>\\n798:export function GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {\\n805:        : \\"A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\\"}</p>\\n808:      <p>Before looking for a word, ask what the rule says. Does it give a rule, cover every member of a group, guarantee a result, or name something required? Each can tell us that if one condition holds, another must hold. A sentence about what usually happens leaves room for exceptions; a conditional rule makes its consequence certain whenever its sufficient condition holds.</p>\\n809:      <p>Read the arrow as <strong>must</strong>. The sufficient condition goes on the left. The necessary condition goes on the right. If the left condition is true, the right condition must be true too. The arrow doesn\'t say which event happened first, and it doesn\'t prove that one event caused the other.</p>\\n821:        <p>Other sentences guarantee an outcome: “A silver seal ensures the painting has been inspected” or “A silver seal implies the painting has been inspected.” Here, the guaranteed outcome is inspection. Ensures and implies express the arrow between the two complete ideas. Leads to, will, and always can also express a definite result in a rule, but the word alone doesn\'t settle the meaning. “The studio will open tomorrow” gives us no sufficient condition to put on the left.</p>\\n824:        <p>A useful way to read the whole rule is: <strong>the left condition requires the right condition</strong>. Suppose borrowing a studio camera requires a current membership. What is required for borrowing? A current membership. That\'s the necessary condition.</p>\\n825:        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>Borrowing a studio camera requires a current membership.</blockquote><p>Requires names what borrowing needs: a current membership. An expired membership would not meet that requirement.</p></div><div><blockquote>A current membership is required to borrow a studio camera.</blockquote><p>The same required idea now comes first. Moving it in the rule hasn\'t changed what borrowing requires.</p></div></div>\\n828:          <p>No. Membership is something borrowing requires. Mina could have the membership and never borrow a camera. The rule tells us what we need if she borrows; having what she needs doesn\'t tell us she did.</p>\\n830:        <p>Notice the meaning shared by <strong>requires, necessitates, depends on,</strong> and <strong>relies on</strong> when they express a strict requirement. If borrowing depends on having a current membership, borrowing still requires membership. “Depends partly on” may describe an influence instead, so read the whole claim before treating it as a conditional.</p>\\n831:        <p>You may also hear the requirement as “In order to borrow a camera, you must have a current membership.” The goal is borrowing; the required condition is membership. “Only people with a current membership borrow a camera” and “You borrow a camera only if you have a current membership” keep membership required. Neither tells us that every member borrows one.</p>\\n833:        <p>You won\'t always see a familiar word. Find what the rule says is required <strong>for the other condition</strong>. Then check that your arrow keeps that requirement on the right.</p>\\n843:        <blockquote>Entry requires a current pass.</blockquote>\\n852:      <blockquote>{first ? \'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.\' : \'Entry requires a current pass.\'}</blockquote>\\n855:        : \\"A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. Shortening a label should save writing while keeping what the rule requires.\\"}</p>\\n858:        : [\'This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.\', \'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the rule requires.\']}/>\\n860:    <Section title={first ? \'Read the only as a complete phrase\' : \'Compare only with the only\'}>\\n862:        <blockquote>The only people admitted are members.</blockquote>\\n863:        <p>The only what? The only people admitted. The complete group is admitted people. If someone is admitted, the rule tells us they\'re a member.</p>\\n866:        <Check prompt=\\"The only notebooks kept on this shelf are hardback. Which diagram keeps the rule?\\" options={[\'Hardback → Kept on shelf\', \'Kept on shelf → Hardback\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You made being hardback enough for a place on the shelf. The rule limits the notebooks kept there; it does not put every hardback notebook on this shelf.\', \'A notebook kept on this shelf has to be hardback. A hardback notebook on another shelf would also fit the rule, so this direction keeps what the rule actually says.\']}/>\\n869:        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>Only climbers with a permit reach the summit.</blockquote><p>Only points to climbers with a permit. A permit is required for every climber who reaches the summit, so has a permit goes right.</p></div><div><blockquote>The only climbers who reach the summit are climbers with a permit.</blockquote><p>The only points to climbers who reach the summit. Reaching it is enough to prove a permit, so reaches the summit goes left.</p></div></div>\\n886:      <Check prompt={first ? \'A report is reviewed if submitted before noon. Which is the same rule?\' : \'A student graduates only if the project is completed. Which is the same rule?\'} options={first ? [\'If reviewed, it was submitted before noon.\', \'If submitted before noon, it is reviewed.\'] : [\'If the student graduates, the project was completed.\', \'If the project is completed, the student graduates.\']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first\\n888:        : [\'This keeps project completion as a requirement for graduation. It does not turn completing the project into a guarantee of graduation.\', \'You turned the only-if requirement into a guarantee. Graduation requires completion, but the rule allows a completed project without graduation.\']}/>\\n889:      <p>The guided practice asks you to write your reasoning with the method beside you. Use the explanation to inspect the direction and complete conditions, not only whether you chose the same answer.</p>\\n899:    <figcaption>A conditional tells us what must be true if the first condition is true. It doesn\'t tell us that the first condition actually is true.</figcaption>\\n904:    <div className=\\"co-role-compass\\"><div><span>Enough</span><strong>Sign up for the fun run</strong><small>Proves getting a T-shirt</small></div><div><span>Required</span><strong>Get a T-shirt</strong><small>The result signing up requires</small></div></div>\\n915:    <p className=\\"co-hero-sentence\\">Entry requires a <strong>current pass.</strong></p>\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6124c7","wall_time_seconds":0.335417,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":151,"output":"C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/.agents/skills/bootcamp-builder/SKILL.md:43:- **Teaching voice:** From the canonical ai-os vault root, read `wiki/teaching-voice.md`, `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`, and `wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md` before authoring or revising LSAT material. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 14.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"0b8a4e","wall_time_seconds":0.3381014,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5095,"output":"# Copy corrections\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery student-facing wording fix, with the reason it was wrong. Read this before\\r\\nwriting or editing copy for LSAT U so the same mistake is not made twice.\\r\\n\\r\\nCompanion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This\\r\\nsays what has actually gone wrong and why.\\r\\n\\r\\nStarted 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a\\r\\ncorrection is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The rules these corrections came from\\r\\n\\r\\nThree rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was\\r\\nenforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface\\r\\nadded later starts uncovered.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.\\r\\n- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`\\r\\n  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.\\r\\n- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nGuards now live with the content rather than with each component:\\r\\n`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every\\r\\nsurface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contrapositive and arrow lessons: cut the sophistry (2026-10-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the first contrapositive lesson as \'terribly done\' and then rejected the rewrite for \'a lot of sophistry\'. Rules from the two rounds:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Say what it is first, in plain words, before any example.** Name the idea, say what it states, then show it. The contrapositive is the **same rule said a different way**. The two are **logically equivalent**: if one is true, the other is true, and you can use either. Lead with that. Source: his own teaching, [[contrapositive-teaching]].\\r\\n- **No metaphor-of-obligation language.** Never write that a rule \'makes a promise\', that the left side \'depends on\' the right, or that it has \'nothing to stand on\'. Say what is true and why, in the plainest reasoning: if the left side had happened, the right side would have come with it.\\r\\n- **Keep it natural, intuitive and personable.** Contractions, short sentences, a teacher thinking next to the student. No lecture-hall phrasing. If a sentence sounds like it is trying to sound wise, cut it.\\r\\n- **Name the lesson by its topic.** \'Contrapositive\', not \'Write and read the contrapositive\'.\\r\\n- **A lesson explains before it drills.** Order for a concept lesson: what it is, see it work, how to do it, what it is not, the steps in short, why it matters. Do not open with an example or a question.\\r\\n- **Must arrow note must say:** what the arrow says, where to start reading (the left), and what the arrow is for. Not just which side is sufficient.\\r\\n- **A card front asks the question, the back gives the answer first.** The answer is the large line. Be specific: \'Can you read a rule from right to left?\', not \'Can you reverse the arrow?\'. Never \'the other one\'.\\r\\n- **Fix the vague word, not the sentence.** \'Reverse\' and \'the other one\' were both rejected as not specific. Say what the student actually does or what the condition actually is.\\r\\n- **Never call the contrapositive \'the one other thing\' a rule tells you.** Joshua (2026-10-11): it is not the only other thing you know. Say the two are equivalent and interchangeable: know one, you know the other. Do not claim a closed list of what else is known.\\r\\n- **Do not use the raffle (win the raffle, get a bicycle) as the example in new contrapositive or arrow material.** Pick a fresh everyday example for each lesson. The contrapositive note now uses an elevator: if it is moving, its doors are closed. The older course text still uses the raffle; replace it as those lessons are revisited.\\r\\n- **Contrapositive lesson (Joshua, 2026-10-11, final): no thought experiment, no guess box, no flip card.** An earlier coat-and-guess card confused him (\'I don\'t know what you did about that coat example\'). Follow the source HTML\'s explanation of the contrapositive directly, in close language, improving only where it can be made clearer: recap the rule, define it up front (if the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too), one first-person example, \'found within the original, waiting to be recognized\', logically equivalent and interchangeable, four-situations table, write it with the squiggle and flip and negate, what it is not, read it don\'t always write it, why it matters. Do not use the source\'s own examples (coffee, breathing/air). Current example: ceiling light and power.\\r\\n- **Say \'left side of the arrow\' and \'right side of the arrow\'** in explanations of the arrow and the contrapositive.\\r\\n- **Never mention Logic Games.** Only LR and RC exist.\\r\\n- **Source cautions are good, keep them:** do not say the rule and its contrapositive are \'all you know\', and do not say to \'ignore the conditional\'. Say the rule settles nothing in the other two situations.\\r\\n- **Say \'must\', not just \'is\', in a rule\'s necessary side (Joshua, 2026-10-11):** \'If the ceiling light is on, then the power MUST be on.\' The must is the point of the arrow.\\r\\n- **\'Foreign\' is an awkward word** (\'it isn\'t some foreign process\'). Use plain, personable phrasing in his Intro to LR voice: a plain claim, a contrast, short stacked landings (\'No power, no light.\'), \'Here\'s the thing\', direct \'you\', contractions, dry wit.\\r\\n- **Emphasis may be a little louder in the contrapositive lesson:** a slightly cheeky exclamation on the key word (\'They\'re interchangeable!\') with a one-time punch-in and glow, plus \'Can\'t happen!\' on the forbidden case. Main Conclusion copy has no exclamation marks in teaching text, so keep them to one or two per lesson.\\r\\n- **Bring lessons to life, but let motion carry the reasoning:** pulse, glow, a punch on the key word, sides that swap and a \'not\' that pops on, toggles that show the four situations. Every animation needs a still, readable equivalent and must stop under reduced motion. Existing pieces to reuse: DrillDemo, HypotheticalFigure (data-in-view), SufficientConditionExperience, indicator-word-deck flip card.\\r\\n- **Never show negation as a squiggle (~) beside an idea (Joshua, 2026-10-11, \'never\').** Negation is either the word \'not\' or a strikethrough line drawn through the idea itself. Applies to every lesson, diagram, drill and animation. Fixed so far: the contrapositive note and its flip-and-negate animation. Still using ~: reference sequence diagrams, sign patterns, worksheet drills and symbol answers (see the next fix pass).\\r\\n- **Negation line runs diagonally from the idea\'s top left corner to its bottom right corner (Joshua, 2026-10-11).** Use it in written lines and tables as well as animations, not only boxes. Also write the contrapositive out as a plain-English sentence early in the explanation.\\r\\n- **No squiggle anywhere, answer choices included (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** He still saw ~ in the multiple-choice options and the reference practice below the lesson. Every ~ in a string is rendered through the Notation component so it draws as the struck idea; a test now fails if ~ appears on the contrapositive page. Check practice buttons and answer keys, not only lesson prose.\\r\\n- **Wrong must look wrong (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Reverse and inverse are shown as red cards with an X badge and the word \\"Wrong\\", beside one green \\"Right\\" card for the contrapositive. Do not put wrong and right in the same plain table. Also: no repeated content on a page (the light and power sentences were said three times), and visuals must look sleek and high end.\\r\\n- **Questions must be unmistakable; no pale green; no broken sentences; every line earns its place (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** The \\"what the rule tells you\\" interactive did not make clear what it was asking. Now: a navy panel, a \\"Your turn\\" tag, the rule, and one big question (\\"Which ONE of these four worlds can\'t happen?\\"), with four tappable worlds. Colour carries meaning (amber = sufficient idea, teal = necessary idea, and the colour travels with the idea when sides flip). Sentences are never split with line breaks or orphaned (balanced wrapping, one card per sentence). \\"Logically equivalent\\" pulses. Cut anything that restates what the page already said (the 4-row table, the raffle recap, the second plain-English line, the repeated light-and-power blockquote). End the lesson with checks that test use of the idea, including one that tests the reverse trap.\\r\\n- **Interactive steps say what to click (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Label them \\"Step 1. Click here to ...\\" and \\"Step 2. Click here to ...\\", not \\"Next step\\" / \\"Play it again\\". The student drives; nothing autoplays. The next step to click glows to invite the click.\\r\\n- **Show the rule, do not just diagram it (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** The original and contrapositive are two tabs on one stage with a real light bulb and switches. Original tab: flip the light switch on and the power node is forced on. Contrapositive tab: cut the power and the bulb is blocked; \\"Try the light switch anyway\\" does nothing. Concept pairs get something to operate, not a static pair of boxes.\\r\\n- **Simple beats clever (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** The first light bulb stage had a diagram row, sentence, two icon boxes, a wire, state chips, a switch, a bonus button and a message. He called it too complicated and messy. Rule: one idea per interactive, one control, one sentence. The tab itself carries the diagram; two plain lamps show the state; no wires, chips or extra buttons.\\r\\n- **Raffle retired from the contrapositive page (2026-10-12).** The \\"Try the four patterns\\" practice used the raffle. It now has four fresh rules, one per place a \\"not\\" can sit (guest list and wristband, shop and shutters, gate and dog, stamped ticket), with reasoned feedback that names the reverse and the inverse. The raffle and bicycle still run through the earlier reference steps, the opening lessons, the media and the shared foundations source (about 300 mentions); those need their own pass and the generated bundle rebuilt.\\r\\n- **Never say \\"patterns\\" or \\"move\\" to students (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Both read as awkward jargon. Say \\"four rules\\", \\"try it on\\", \\"change\\", \\"flip the sides\\". Practice questions get the same visual quality as the rest of the page: one rule at a time on the navy panel, colour-coded idea pills, large answer tiles, instant reasoned feedback, progress dots. The weakest-looking part of a page gets rebuilt, not patched.\\r\\n- **Words:** \'Correct\', not \'Right\', on a correct answer. Bold the word \'if\' where the point is that the rule says what follows *if* something is true.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Contrapositive page approved as the gold standard (Joshua, 2026-10-12).** Full do and don\'t list, page shape and build process live in `.claude/skills/bootcamp-builder/references/teaching-preferences.md` under \\"Gold standard\\". New rules from the last pass: cut whole sections when a page is too long; one main example plus one fresh simple example, never repeated; show rule and result as if-then sentences; quiz ideas are short phrases, not letters; quiz explanations are one sentence (right answer) or \\"Not that one.\\" plus one specific reason (wrong answer); no paragraph or tag line before a quiz; the quiz title is just \\"Pop Quiz\\", centered, with an effect not used elsewhere on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\nStill to reconcile (found 2026-10-11, not Joshua decisions): \'establish\' is banned below but his approved necessary copy uses it; the raffle was approved for the opening and later rejected as the sufficient anchor; see `.claude/skills/bootcamp-builder/references/teaching-preferences.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.\\r\\n\\r\\n2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same relationship with if at the beginning and in the middle, and show necessary targets both before and after requirement language. Neither sentence position nor the words immediately following the indicator determine placement. Preserve if versus only if as different complete expressions.\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact and rule definitions must explain the inference (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Two connecting rules produce another conditional rule without establishing a case exists. Adding a fact to the chain is a separate step. Explain all four fact situations and a hidden chain revealed by contraposition rather than leaving them implicit in an interactive model. Rules include general logical relationships, not only policies or obligations.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Boxed `L -> R` → bold teaching notation **L → R** (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the recap\'s small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Concept lesson\\" → the actual reasoning title (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe recap preview exposed an internal label above the student lesson. Joshua explicitly rejected it. Use a title such as \\"Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\" and headings that teach the reasoning. Keep production labels, version metadata, and design commentary in private records. Check the rendered HTML, since its shell can introduce wording absent from the Markdown source.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Recall Rephrase Colour\\" → \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\"\\r\\nThe door\'s heading was hardcoded to a name the product does not have, one line\\r\\nunder a chip reading \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\". \\"Rephrase\\" belongs to a different\\r\\nActivity, Conditional Rephrase. A test asserted the wrong name, which is why\\r\\n2,600 passing tests never caught it.\\r\\n**Lesson:** when a test pins a string, check the string is right, not just that\\r\\nit matches.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Intake only. Nothing comes back out yet…\\" → plain instruction\\r\\nClinical and abstract, and the sentence then told her to read \\"with full\\r\\nintonation\\" while step one is silent reading. The clause that made intonation\\r\\ninternal, \\"hear it naturally in your head\\", had been dropped in an earlier edit.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a word like \\"intake\\" reads as jargon. And when copy is trimmed,\\r\\ncheck what the trimmed clause was load-bearing for.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Three beats through the stimulus\\" → \\"Three steps\\"\\r\\n\\"Beat\\" is the internal code name for one of the four method steps. The glossary\\r\\nsays students see **step**. The interface ten pixels below labelled the same four\\r\\nthings Step 1 to Step 4, so the page used two names for one thing.\\r\\n**Lesson:** never let an implementation name reach the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"22 pieces to read\\" → line removed\\r\\nA **piece** is one chunk, roughly a sentence, and the instructions on that same\\r\\npage use the word that way. The count was of stimuli, so \\"22 pieces\\" read as 22\\r\\nsentences.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a unit word must mean one thing per surface. Check what a word\\r\\nalready means on the page before reusing it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"say it back\\" → \\"tell it back\\", \\"The Read\\" → \\"The Recall\\"\\r\\nThe library door still carried both retired names. \\"The Read\\" was step four\'s\\r\\nformer name and collided with step one, which is why it was renamed.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a rename is not finished until every row carries it. The door was\\r\\nstale for months because it is a different database row from the sections.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"then a fourth\\" → \\"then a fourth step\\"\\r\\nThe noun sat three words back and the reader had to carry it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"once you have got all of it\\" → \\"once you have read all of it\\"\\r\\nAmbiguous between having read it and having remembered it, in the one clause\\r\\nthat triggers step four.\\r\\n**Lesson:** precision matters most where the sentence is an instruction.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The three-fact notice → one fact\\r\\n\\"Nothing is recording yet, the microphone opens on Start, and the section\\r\\nrecords in one take that you cannot pause.\\" Two of those are said again in step\\r\\nfour and in the long version, so she skipped the paragraph.\\r\\n**Lesson:** say it once, in the place it matters.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The method, told twice → the long version collapsed\\r\\nThe page rendered four numbered steps and then the whole written briefing saying\\r\\nthe same thing at length, both open. She read the method twice and could not\\r\\ntell which one to follow.\\r\\n**Lesson:** two explanations of one thing is worse than either alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"PT105.S1 · 24 stimuli · about 25 min\\" → removed\\r\\nThe section was already the second line of the title. Do not reword it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Booking emails: hand over the link, not the pass settings (2026-09-08)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** \\"70 minutes each, and no expiration date, so there\'s no rush to use them.\\"\\r\\n**Keep:** \\"You\'re set up on LSAT U with 10 sessions.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nSession length and expiry are admin fields. The credit count is worth saying because\\r\\nit tells her how much she has. Graduation requires completion, but the rule allows a completed project without graduation.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>The guided practice asks you to write your reasoning with the method beside you. \'present\' : \'absent\'}</span></span></th>\\r\\n        <td data-label=\\"What follows?\\"><span className=\\"fs-follows\\">{row.settled ? <Check size={18} strokeWidth={3} aria-hidden=\\"true\\"/> : <Minus size={18} strokeWidth={3} aria-hidden=\\"true\\"/>}{row.follows}</span></td>\\r\\n        <td data-label=\\"The pool rule\\"><span className=\\"fs-tag\\">{row.tag}</span><span className=\\"fs-example\\">{row.example}</span></td>\\r\\n      </tr>)}\\r\\n    </tbody>\\r\\n  </table>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst quiz: QuizItem[] = [\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'If a library book is overdue, the borrower gets a reminder text.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Sam’s book is overdue. What follows?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'Nothing. Sam may or may not get a text.\', reason: \'An overdue book is the sufficient condition. When it’s present, the rule does settle something.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Sam gets a reminder text.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Sam returns the book soon.\', reason: \'The rule says nothing about returning the book.\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 1,\\r\\n    answer: \'Sam’s book is overdue, so Sam gets a reminder text.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'If a library book is overdue, the borrower gets a reminder text.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Lena got a reminder text. What follows about his book?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'His book is not overdue.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Nothing. His book may or may not be overdue.\', reason: \'The necessary condition is absent here, and that does settle something.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'His book is overdue.\', reason: \'An overdue book would have brought a text, and Omar got none.\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 0,\\r\\n    answer: \'Omar got no reminder text, so his book isn’t overdue.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    rule: \'If a library book is overdue, the borrower gets a reminder text.\',\\r\\n    question: \'Priya’s book is not overdue. What follows?\',\\r\\n    options: [\\r\\n      { text: \'She gets a reminder text.\', reason: \'Nothing in the rule requires a text here.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'Nothing. She may or may not get a text.\' },\\r\\n      { text: \'She gets no reminder text.\', reason: \'She could still get a text for another reason.\' },\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    correct: 1,\\r\\n    answer: \'Priya’s book isn’t overdue, so the rule doesn’t settle whether she gets a text.\',\\r\\n  },\\r\\n];\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction Section({ title, children }: { title: string; children: React.ReactNode }) {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section\\"><div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}</section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function FourSituationsNote() {\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"When a rule settles something\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ct-definition\\"><strong>A rule settles something in two situations: when the sufficient condition is present, and when the necessary condition is absent.</strong></p>\\r\\n      <p>Take this rule: <strong>If the pool is open, then a lifeguard must be on duty.</strong></p>\\r\\n      <p>The pool is open? Then a lifeguard must be on duty. That’s the original rule. No lifeguard on duty? Then <strong>the pool can’t be open.</strong> That’s the contrapositive. In the other two situations, the rule settles nothing.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Don’t fall for the necessary condition\\">\\r\\n      <p>The LSAT will give you a rule, then tell you the necessary condition is present. A lifeguard is on duty. You’ll want to conclude the pool is open. Don’t. The lifeguard could be there for a training session with the pool closed.</p>\\r\\n      <p>When the necessary condition is present, <PulseWord>the rule settles nothing.</PulseWord> The pool could be open or closed.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Four situations\\">\\r\\n      <p>Any fact about the rule puts you in one of four situations.</p>\\r\\n      <SituationChart/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Ask where the fact lands\\">\\r\\n      <p>When a rule comes with a fact, check which side the fact is about before you conclude anything. If the fact lands in one of the two situations that settle nothing, look for help somewhere else in the argument.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function FourSituationsQuiz() {\\r\\n  return <QuickQuiz items={quiz} idPrefix=\\"fs\\"/>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]
